# A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models

Ali Forootani^(\*) ^(†)^(†)thanks: Ali Forootani is with Helmholtz
Centre for Environmental Research - UFZ, Permoserstraße 15, 04318
Leipzig, Germany, email: ali.forootani@ufz.de, aliforootani@ieee.org.

###### Abstract

Mathematical reasoning and optimization are fundamental to artificial
intelligence and computational problem-solving. Recent advancements in
large language models have significantly enhanced AI-driven mathematical
reasoning, theorem proving, and optimization techniques. This survey
explores the evolution of mathematical problem-solving in AI, from early
statistical learning approaches to the latest deep learning-based and
transformer-driven methodologies. We review the capabilities of
pre-trained language models and Large Language Models in handling
arithmetic operations, complex mathematical reasoning, theorem proving,
and structured symbolic computation.

A key focus is on how Large Language Models integrate with optimization
and control frameworks, including mixed-integer programming, linear
quadratic control, and multi-agent optimization strategies. We discuss
how Large Language Models assist in problem formulation, constraint
generation, and heuristic search, thereby bridging the gap between
theoretical mathematical reasoning and practical optimization
applications. Additionally, we analyze various enhancement techniques,
including Chain-of-Thought reasoning, instruction tuning, and
tool-augmented approaches that improve Large Language Model’s
problem-solving capabilities.

Despite these advancements, Large Language Models face challenges in
numerical precision, logical consistency, and verification of
mathematical proofs. We highlight emerging trends in hybrid
neural-symbolic reasoning, structured prompt engineering, and multi-step
self-correction mechanisms aimed at improving Large Language Model
performance in mathematical and optimization tasks. Future research
should focus on refining interpretability, integrating domain-specific
solvers, and ensuring the robustness of AI-driven decision-making
frameworks. This survey provides a comprehensive review of the current
landscape and future directions of mathematical reasoning and
optimization with Large Language Models, offering insights into their
potential applications across engineering, finance, and scientific
research.

###### Index Terms: 

Mathematical Reasoning, Optimization, Large Language Models (LLMs),
Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Reasoning, Theorem Proving, Symbolic Computation,
Hybrid Neural-Symbolic Methods, AI-Driven Decision-Making, Control and
Optimization, Linear Programming.

## I Introduction

Mathematics serves as a cornerstone of human cognition, underpinning a
vast array of disciplines, including the natural sciences, engineering,
medicine, finance, computer science, and the social sciences. In the
field of natural language processing (NLP), the development of
computational models capable of autonomously solving mathematical word
problems has been an area of significant research interest since the
early 1960s \[[1](#bib.bib1), [2](#bib.bib2), [3](#bib.bib3),
[4](#bib.bib4)\]. This pursuit is not merely about solving arithmetic
and algebraic expressions but also about advancing general reasoning
mechanisms, which are fundamental to achieving artificial general
intelligence (AGI) \[[5](#bib.bib5)\]. By tackling the complexities of
mathematical reasoning, researchers aim to push the boundaries of AI
systems’ logical deduction capabilities and their ability to understand
and manipulate symbolic knowledge.

Early approaches to solving mathematical word problems primarily relied
on statistical learning techniques \[[6](#bib.bib6), [7](#bib.bib7),
[8](#bib.bib8), [9](#bib.bib9)\]. Over time, more sophisticated
methodologies emerged, incorporating semantic parsing and structured
representations \[[7](#bib.bib7), [10](#bib.bib10), [11](#bib.bib11),
[12](#bib.bib12), [13](#bib.bib13)\]. These innovations laid the
foundation for modern deep learning-based mathematical problem-solving,
where neural networks have become a powerful tool for automating complex
mathematical reasoning tasks \[[5](#bib.bib5), [14](#bib.bib14)\].

In recent years, the field has witnessed unprecedented advancements,
largely driven by the rise of powerful pre-trained language models
(PLMs) and large language models (LLMs) \[[15](#bib.bib15),
[16](#bib.bib16)\]. These models, including BERT \[[17](#bib.bib17)\],
RoBERTa \[[18](#bib.bib18)\], BART \[[19](#bib.bib19)\], GPT-1
\[[20](#bib.bib20)\], and GPT-2 \[[21](#bib.bib21)\], have demonstrated
remarkable capabilities in learning from large-scale textual corpora,
thereby acquiring general linguistic and numerical reasoning skills.

To enhance the mathematical reasoning abilities of PLMs, researchers
have explored various techniques, including pre-training and fine-tuning
on specialized mathematical datasets \[[22](#bib.bib22),
[23](#bib.bib23), [24](#bib.bib24)\]. Several models, such as
GenBERT\[[23](#bib.bib23)\], NF-NSM\[[24](#bib.bib24)\],
MathBERT\[[25](#bib.bib25)\], and LISA \[[26](#bib.bib26)\], integrate
numerical data and mathematical expressions into their training
objectives, enhancing their ability to perform symbolic and numerical
reasoning. Moreover, architectural innovations such as specialized loss
functions and task-specific modules have been proposed to further refine
PLM-based mathematical comprehension \[[27](#bib.bib27),
[28](#bib.bib28), [29](#bib.bib29), [30](#bib.bib30)\].

The emergence of large-scale LLMs, exemplified by GPT-4
\[[31](#bib.bib31)\], has significantly transformed mathematical
problem-solving. These models have demonstrated remarkable
generalization across various NLP tasks, benefiting from key techniques
such as in-context learning \[[32](#bib.bib32), [33](#bib.bib33),
[34](#bib.bib34)\] and instruction learning \[[35](#bib.bib35),
[36](#bib.bib36)\]. Notably, research by Wang et al.
\[[37](#bib.bib37)\] suggests that LLMs with over 100 billion
parameters—such as GPT-3 (175 billion) \[[33](#bib.bib33)\] and PaLM
(540 billion) \[[38](#bib.bib38)\]—can effectively solve complex
mathematical problems through chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning when
provided with well-structured demonstrations \[[15](#bib.bib15)\].

Advancements in CoT methodologies have further improved the reasoning
capabilities of LLMs, leading to the development of structured reasoning
techniques, programmatic reasoning frameworks, and dynamic CoT models
\[[39](#bib.bib39), [40](#bib.bib40), [41](#bib.bib41),
[42](#bib.bib42)\]. Additionally, tool-augmented models that integrate
external symbolic manipulation systems have been proposed to enhance
mathematical problem-solving performance \[[43](#bib.bib43),
[44](#bib.bib44), [45](#bib.bib45)\]. Such approaches highlight the
potential of combining neural and symbolic reasoning to achieve more
robust AI systems capable of higher-order mathematical cognition.

Several surveys have reviewed different aspects of mathematical
reasoning in LLMs. Notably:

- •
  \[[46](#bib.bib46)\] provides a comprehensive survey of deep learning
  methodologies for mathematical reasoning (DL4Math).
- •
  \[[47](#bib.bib47)\] explores general reasoning mechanisms in language
  models, covering arithmetic, commonsense, logical, symbolic, and
  multi-modal reasoning (LM4Reasoning).
- •
  \[[48](#bib.bib48)\] presents an analytical review of chain-of-thought
  (CoT) reasoning methodologies.
- •
  \[[49](#bib.bib49)\] and \[[50](#bib.bib50)\] offer surveys on
  pre-trained language models (PLMs) and LLMs, respectively.
- •
  \[[51](#bib.bib51)\] reviews multi-modal LLMs for mathematical
  reasoning (MMLM4Math).

Overall, the fusion of deep learning techniques, structured reasoning
approaches, and tool-augmented models continues to drive rapid progress
in AI-driven mathematical problem-solving. While existing methods have
demonstrated impressive performance, fundamental challenges remain,
particularly in areas such as formal proof generation, theorem proving,
and real-world mathematical applications. As research continues to push
the boundaries of AI’s mathematical reasoning capabilities, future
advancements will likely be characterized by improved interpretability,
robustness, and the seamless integration of symbolic and neural
reasoning paradigms.

### I-A Bridging Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with LLMs

Mathematical reasoning forms the foundation of logical inference,
problem-solving, and numerical computation in artificial intelligence
\[[52](#bib.bib52)\]. Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated
remarkable capabilities in handling mathematical reasoning tasks,
including theorem proving, algebraic manipulation, and structured
numerical problem-solving. Through advancements such as Chain-of-Thought
(CoT) prompting, programmatic reasoning, and tool-augmented inference,
LLMs have shown substantial progress in solving complex mathematical
problems \[[53](#bib.bib53)\]. However, the transition from theoretical
reasoning to practical applications, such as optimization and control,
requires an additional layer of adaptability where models can not only
deduce but also generate and refine solutions iteratively.

Optimization and control problems often demand a structured approach
where solutions must satisfy strict mathematical constraints while
achieving predefined objectives. In scenarios such as mixed-integer
programming (MIP) and linear programming (LP), traditional solvers rely
on well-established algorithms, including branch-and-bound methods and
gradient-based optimization. While LLMs may not replace these solvers
outright, they can significantly enhance the modeling process by
formulating problem statements, generating initial constraints, and
suggesting heuristic improvements \[[54](#bib.bib54)\]. By leveraging
their extensive pretraining in symbolic mathematics and structured
problem-solving, LLMs can aid domain experts in defining well-posed
optimization problems and verifying computational solutions.

A crucial advantage of LLMs in this domain lies in their ability to
process natural language descriptions of complex optimization scenarios
and convert them into structured mathematical formulations
\[[55](#bib.bib55)\]. For instance, in the context of battery energy
storage system (BESS) optimization, an LLM can help translate high-level
policy constraints—such as cost minimization, peak shaving, or energy
arbitrage—into a solvable mathematical model. Similarly, in control
applications like linear quadratic regulation (LQR), LLMs can assist in
deriving optimal control laws by structuring the necessary state-space
representations and computing feedback gains iteratively.

By integrating mathematical reasoning with optimization and control
strategies, LLMs present an opportunity to bridge the gap between
abstract theoretical models and real-world engineering applications
\[[56](#bib.bib56)\]. Their ability to automate certain aspects of model
formulation, constraint generation, and heuristic search makes them
valuable tools for decision-making in complex systems. Future research
should focus on refining their numerical precision, enhancing symbolic
computation capabilities, and improving their ability to interface
seamlessly with existing optimization solvers. This synergy between
mathematical reasoning and optimization will pave the way for more
intelligent, interpretable, and efficient decision-support systems in
engineering and scientific domains.

This paper is organized as follows: In
Section [II](#S2 "II Mathematical Tasks ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models"),
we introduce the types of mathematical tasks that motivate the use of
language models.
Section [III](#S3 "III Pre-trained Language Models (PLMs)-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
presents pre-trained language model (PLM)-based approaches for
mathematical reasoning, with a particular focus on Non-Autoregressive
Language Models discussed in
Section [III-B](#S3.SS2 "III-B Non-Autoregressive Language Models ‣ III Pre-trained Language Models (PLMs)-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models").
Section [IV](#S4 "IV LLM-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
explores large language model (LLM)-based methods tailored for
mathematical reasoning. In
Section [V](#S5 "V Comparison of Mathematical Large Language Models ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models"),
we compare various mathematical LLMs, followed by a performance analysis
in
Section [VI](#S6 "VI Performance Analysis of Mathematical Language Models ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models").
Section [VIII](#S8 "VIII LLMs for Time Series Analysis: Challenges, Methods, and Future Directions ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
investigates the application of LLMs to time series analysis, outlining
the associated challenges, methodologies, and future directions. A
technical comparison of commonly used LLMs is provided in
Section [IX](#S9 "IX Comparison of commonly used LLMs based on their technical specifications ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models").
Section [X](#S10 "X Application of LLms on optimization and control ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
discusses the role of LLMs in optimization and control.
Section [XI](#S11 "XI Challenges and Further Directions ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
highlights open challenges and outlines potential research directions.
Finally, conclusions are drawn in
Section [XII](#S12 "XII Conclusion ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models").

## II Mathematical Tasks

In this section, we categorize mathematical tasks into two primary
domains: mathematical calculation and mathematical reasoning.

### II-A Mathematical Calculation

The emergence of LMs has significantly influenced computational
research, particularly in performing arithmetic operations. Early LMs
exhibited only rudimentary arithmetic capabilities, encoding numbers as
mere textual representations. However, as these models evolved, they
demonstrated the ability to develop arithmetic skills through
fine-tuning on specialized datasets, even without explicitly designed
numerical representations \[[57](#bib.bib57)\].

#### II-A1 Arithmetic Representation

Initially, numerical values were often omitted, oversimplified, or
treated as ordinary text tokens, leading to suboptimal performance on
mathematical tasks. For instance, BERT performs five times worse when
the expected answer is a numerical value rather than a textual span in
the DROP benchmark \[[58](#bib.bib58)\]. This limitation highlighted the
necessity of refining how LMs process and represent numbers.

Recent research has proposed several techniques to enhance numerical
representation within LMs:

- •
  GenBERT \[[23](#bib.bib23)\] tokenizes numbers at the digit level and
  fine-tunes on arithmetic word problems.
- •
  In \[[59](#bib.bib59)\] a technique is introduced that converts
  numbers into scientific notation (e.g., 314.1 as 3141\[EXP\]2) to
  facilitate better numerical understanding.
- •
  Studies such as \[[60](#bib.bib60)\] and \[[61](#bib.bib61)\]
  integrate digit embeddings into a single embedding that represents
  entire numbers more effectively.
- •
  In \[[62](#bib.bib62)\] a method is proposed to leverage digit-RNN and
  exponent embeddings, emphasizing the exponent while disregarding the
  mantissa for improved numerical processing.
- •
  GOAT \[[36](#bib.bib36)\] introduced Consistent Tokenization to
  enhance the numerical representation consistency within LMs.

These techniques have collectively improved the way LMs interpret,
store, and manipulate numerical values, significantly boosting their
effectiveness in arithmetic reasoning.

#### II-A2 Arithmetic Calculation

The ability of LMs to perform arithmetic computations has been a widely
studied topic. Early research primarily focused on fundamental
operations such as addition and subtraction \[[63](#bib.bib63),
[64](#bib.bib64)\], whereas more recent studies have expanded into
complex operations like multi-digit multiplication \[[65](#bib.bib65)\].

Key findings in arithmetic calculation include:

- •
  In \[[66](#bib.bib66)\] arithmetic capabilities across various LMs,
  including GPT-4 \[[31](#bib.bib31)\], Galactica \[[67](#bib.bib67)\],
  and LLaMA \[[68](#bib.bib68)\] hase been evaluated.
- •
  In \[[69](#bib.bib69)\] has been demonstrated that specialized prompt
  engineering can significantly improve addition accuracy, though
  multiplication remains challenging for numbers exceeding seven digits.
- •
  In \[[70](#bib.bib70)\] length generalization has been investigated in
  arithmetic using relative position embeddings and training set
  priming.
- •
  ScratchpadGPT \[[71](#bib.bib71)\] leveraged Chain-of-Thought (CoT)
  prompting to enhance addition accuracy.
- •
  In GOAT \[[36](#bib.bib36)\] supervised instruction tuning has been
  applied to improve performance on large-integer arithmetic tasks.
- •
  MathGLM \[[35](#bib.bib35)\] decomposed complex arithmetic expressions
  into simpler subproblems, enhancing model precision.

These advancements illustrate the growing sophistication of LMs in
arithmetic computations and their potential applications in
computational mathematics.

### II-B Mathematical Reasoning

Mathematical reasoning is a critical capability in artificial
intelligence, enabling models to understand, explain, and solve complex
mathematical problems. Research in this domain has focused on two
primary areas: math problem-solving and theorem proving.

#### II-B1 Math Problem Solving

Math problem-solving requires LMs to apply logical reasoning and
computational models to solve mathematical problems at various levels of
complexity. Generally, mathematical problems fall into two categories:

- •
  Contextualized problems: These involve understanding a given scenario,
  translating it into a mathematical problem, and generating a
  step-by-step solution.
- •
  Direct mathematical problems: These require direct computation of a
  solution without the need for additional contextual interpretation.

Several notable advancements in this domain include:

- •
  MathPrompter \[[53](#bib.bib53)\] utilizes GPT-3 DaVinci for solving
  mathematical word problems (MWPs).
- •
  In \[[72](#bib.bib72)\] Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RFT) has been
  proposed to enhance mathematical reasoning capabilities in LMs.
- •
  MetaMath \[[73](#bib.bib73)\] generates synthetic mathematical
  problems to facilitate self-improvement.
- •
  WizardMath \[[74](#bib.bib74)\] applies evolutionary instruction
  tuning to refine LM’s mathematical reasoning skills.
- •
  MathAttack \[[75](#bib.bib75)\] evaluates the robustness of LMs
  against adversarial examples in mathematical problem-solving.
- •
  LLEMMA \[[76](#bib.bib76)\] is an open-source LM designed specifically
  for mathematical reasoning.

These models illustrate the ongoing effort to refine LM’s ability to
reason mathematically and solve increasingly complex problems.

#### II-B2 Theorem Proving

Theorem proving (TP) is a challenging AI task that involves verifying
mathematical statements using formal logic and structured reasoning.
Recent research has investigated the ability of LMs to engage in theorem
proving by leveraging deep learning techniques and formal proof
verification systems.

Key advancements in this area include:

- •
  GPT-f \[[77](#bib.bib77)\] generates proof strategies using deep
  learning-based methods.
- •
  In \[[78](#bib.bib78)\] incorporating informal proofs into TP models
  to enhance theorem reasoning has been proposed.
- •
  NaturalProofs \[[79](#bib.bib79)\] compiled a comprehensive corpus for
  evaluating theorem-proving models.
- •
  DeepMath \[[80](#bib.bib80)\] and INT \[[81](#bib.bib81)\] assess the
  generalization capabilities of LMs in theorem proving.
- •
  CoqGym \[[82](#bib.bib82)\] provides a dataset of human-written proofs
  to aid LM training in formal theorem proving.
- •
  ASTactic \[[82](#bib.bib82)\] autonomously generates proof strategies
  using deep learning.
- •
  LLEMMA \[[76](#bib.bib76)\] explores auto-formalization and tactic
  prediction for mathematical proof generation.

Despite promising advancements, a significant challenge remains:
hallucination in neural text generation \[[83](#bib.bib83),
[84](#bib.bib84)\]. Errors in logical reasoning can accumulate across
proof steps, leading to incorrect conclusions. This highlights the
urgent need for robust verification mechanisms to ensure mathematical
rigor in AI-generated proofs.

Mathematical reasoning and arithmetic problem-solving represent crucial
steps in improving LMs’ structured reasoning capabilities. While current
research continues to push the boundaries of AI-driven mathematical
understanding, future advancements will likely focus on
interpretability, error reduction, and the seamless integration of
symbolic and neural reasoning approaches.

## III Pre-trained Language Models (PLMs)-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning

PLMs, built on the Transformer architecture \[[85](#bib.bib85)\], have
profoundly influenced Natural Language Processing (NLP) through the
pre-training and fine-tuning paradigm. PLMs have been extensively
utilized in text generation \[[86](#bib.bib86)\] and can be broadly
categorized into two major types: Autoregressive LMs (ALMs), such as
GPT-1 \[[20](#bib.bib20)\] and T5 \[[87](#bib.bib87)\], and
Non-Autoregressive LMs (NALMs), including BERT \[[88](#bib.bib88)\] and
RoBERTa\[[18](#bib.bib18)\]. These architectures have laid the
groundwork for advanced mathematical reasoning models, offering new
capabilities in symbolic manipulation, theorem proving, and arithmetic
problem-solving.

### III-A Autoregressive Language Models

Autoregressive Language Models (ALMs) generate text in a sequential
manner, where each token is predicted based on the preceding tokens.
These models are primarily categorized into two types: causal decoders,
such as GPT-1 \[[20](#bib.bib20)\] and GPT-2 \[[21](#bib.bib21)\], and
encoder-decoder architectures, exemplified by T5 \[[87](#bib.bib87)\].
These architectures have significantly contributed to advancements in
mathematical reasoning within Pretrained Language Models (PLMs).

Several research efforts have focused on adapting autoregressive models
to improve mathematical reasoning capabilities:

- •
  GPT-f \[[77](#bib.bib77)\] explores automated theorem proving,
  demonstrating that large-scale language models can generate formal
  proofs, thereby showcasing their potential for logical deduction.
- •
  The Expression-Pointer Transformer (EPT) \[[89](#bib.bib89)\]
  addresses expression fragmentation issues, significantly enhancing
  equation-solving abilities in symbolic mathematical tasks.
- •
  Generate & Rank \[[90](#bib.bib90)\] introduces a ranking-based
  post-processing mechanism that refines the quality of generated
  solutions for mathematical word problems (MWPs).
- •
  THOR \[[91](#bib.bib91)\] integrates theorem proving within language
  models, leveraging logical reasoning to improve deductive
  capabilities.
- •
  HyperTree Proof Search (HTPS) \[[92](#bib.bib92)\] optimizes proof
  search strategies by incorporating prior results, surpassing GPT-f in
  theorem-proving efficiency and accuracy.
- •
  Galactica \[[67](#bib.bib67)\] enhances performance on mathematical
  reasoning benchmarks, outperforming conventional PLMs on datasets such
  as MMLU \[[93](#bib.bib93)\] and MATH \[[94](#bib.bib94)\] through
  improved pretraining and fine-tuning techniques.

The performance of autoregressive PLMs in mathematical reasoning has
been further refined through the development of specialized datasets and
fine-tuning methodologies:

- •
  MATH-PLM \[[94](#bib.bib94)\] underscores the challenge of scaling
  PLMs for mathematical reasoning and provides benchmark datasets that
  serve as rigorous evaluation tools.
- •
  LISA \[[26](#bib.bib26)\] constructs theorem-proving models by
  leveraging formal proof structures, thereby strengthening structured
  logical reasoning within PLMs.
- •
  PACT \[[95](#bib.bib95)\] introduces self-supervised learning
  techniques to enhance proof-term generation, improving the
  interpretability and accuracy of mathematical proofs.
- •
  Minerva \[[96](#bib.bib96)\] fine-tunes PLMs on high-quality technical
  mathematical content, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on
  various mathematical reasoning tasks.
- •
  LIME \[[97](#bib.bib97)\] proposes a novel pretraining strategy that
  induces mathematical reasoning biases, leading to superior performance
  compared to conventional transformer-based models.

The success of autoregressive LMs in solving mathematical problems can
largely be attributed to their capacity to model complex sequential
dependencies. This makes them particularly well-suited for theorem
proving, symbolic manipulation, and structured logical inference tasks.

### III-B Non-Autoregressive Language Models

Non-Autoregressive LMs (NALMs) differ from their autoregressive
counterparts by generating multiple tokens simultaneously rather than
sequentially. This parallelism significantly reduces dependency on
previously generated tokens, leading to improved efficiency. Models such
as BERT \[[88](#bib.bib88)\] and RoBERTa \[[18](#bib.bib18)\] employ
masked token prediction, allowing them to learn contextual
representations more effectively, making them well-suited for NLP tasks
that require robust comprehension rather than generative capabilities.

In mathematical reasoning, fine-tuned variants of BERT have demonstrated
remarkable performance:

- •
  Aristo \[[22](#bib.bib22)\] adapts BERT for solving scientific exam
  questions, demonstrating its ability to process structured reasoning
  problems.
- •
  GenBERT \[[23](#bib.bib23)\] and NF-NSM \[[24](#bib.bib24)\] enhance
  numerical reasoning by incorporating numerical data into pre-training
  objectives.
- •
  MWP-BERT \[[98](#bib.bib98)\] improves numerical representation in
  mathematical word problems through numeracy-augmented pretraining.
- •
  MathBERT \[[25](#bib.bib25)\] jointly trains on both textual and
  mathematical formula representations, improving mathematical
  comprehension.
- •
  TAGOP \[[27](#bib.bib27)\], MT2Net \[[28](#bib.bib28)\], and
  DeductReasoner \[[29](#bib.bib29)\] leverage PLMs for advanced
  mathematical operations and logical reasoning.
- •
  BERT-TD \[[30](#bib.bib30)\] applies contrastive learning to improve
  mathematical word problem pattern recognition.

Although NALMs are not inherently designed for generative tasks, they
excel in mathematical comprehension due to their bidirectional encoding
capabilities. By leveraging masked language modeling and contextual
token embeddings, these models can capture intricate mathematical
structures and improve equation parsing accuracy.

### III-C Discussion and Future Directions

The adoption of PLM-based approaches in mathematical reasoning has
yielded significant progress, particularly in theorem proving,
arithmetic calculations, and mathematical problem-solving. However,
several challenges remain:

- •
  Numerical Precision: PLMs often struggle with exact arithmetic
  computations due to floating-point representation errors.
- •
  Long-Range Dependencies: Complex mathematical proofs require deep
  reasoning chains, which current models struggle to retain effectively.
- •
  Hallucination in Proof Generation: The tendency of LMs to generate
  incorrect but plausible-looking proofs remains a significant
  limitation.
- •
  Hybrid Neural-Symbolic Methods: Integrating PLMs with symbolic solvers
  could enhance the rigor and correctness of mathematical reasoning.

Future research directions should focus on improving interpretability,
reducing hallucinations, and integrating structured reasoning modules to
ensure mathematical rigor. As PLMs continue to evolve, their role in
automating theorem proving and mathematical modeling is expected to
expand, pushing the boundaries of artificial intelligence in formal
reasoning.

## IV LLM-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning

LLMs have revolutionized text processing and generation, extending their
capabilities to mathematical reasoning \[[68](#bib.bib68),
[31](#bib.bib31)\]. Since mathematics follows structured linguistic and
symbolic rules, LLMs capable of understanding these rules serve as
powerful tools for problem-solving in various mathematical domains
\[[76](#bib.bib76), [74](#bib.bib74)\]. Existing research in LLM-based
mathematical reasoning can be broadly categorized into four key areas:
instruction learning, tool-based methods, fundamental Chain-of-Thought
(CoT) techniques, and advanced CoT methodologies.

### IV-A Instruction Learning

Instruction learning improves mathematical reasoning in LLMs through
structured task formulation, supervised fine-tuning, and in-context
learning.

Instruction Building involves generating structured explanations and
guidance to enhance model capabilities. Auto-explanation
\[[99](#bib.bib99)\] enables LLMs to construct self-explanatory
mathematical datasets, improving problem comprehension. RefAug
\[[100](#bib.bib100)\] leverages GPT-4o to augment mathematical
reasoning data, enriching training samples. Evol-Instruct
\[[101](#bib.bib101)\] introduces an iterative refinement mechanism
where LLMs generate and refine their own instructional prompts, further
enhanced by WizardMath’s \[[74](#bib.bib74)\] RLEIF optimization method.
EURUS \[[102](#bib.bib102)\] integrates CoT \[[15](#bib.bib15)\] with
external tools, optimizing reasoning paths using Direct Preference
Optimization (DPO) \[[103](#bib.bib103)\] and Noise Contrastive
Alignment (NCA) \[[104](#bib.bib104)\].

Instruction Tuning fine-tunes LLMs to align outputs with human intent
\[[105](#bib.bib105)\]. MathGLM \[[35](#bib.bib35)\] and GOAT
\[[36](#bib.bib36)\] improve arithmetic operations through structured
tokenization, while Calculon \[[65](#bib.bib65)\] employs digit
decomposition for numerical precision. PaLM 2-L-Math
\[[106](#bib.bib106)\] demonstrates that combining solution re-ranking
with majority voting enhances performance in mathematical reasoning.
LLEMMA \[[76](#bib.bib76)\] pre-trains on mathematical corpora and
integrates a Python interpreter to execute symbolic computations. More
recent LLMs such as Qwen2.5-MATH \[[107](#bib.bib107)\] and
InternLM-Math \[[108](#bib.bib108)\] employ iterative fine-tuning and
reward modeling to refine mathematical understanding and improve
accuracy.

In-Context Learning (ICL) enhances mathematical reasoning by presenting
task examples during inference without modifying model parameters
\[[33](#bib.bib33)\]. ScratchpadGPT \[[71](#bib.bib71)\] improves
multi-step calculations by generating intermediate reasoning steps.
Codex-math\[[44](#bib.bib44)\] fine-tunes LLMs on code-based
mathematical tasks for automated problem-solving. LAMBADA
\[[109](#bib.bib109)\] introduces backward reasoning, guiding LLMs in
reverse problem-solving, while LogicSolver \[[110](#bib.bib110)\] embeds
algebraic logic into in-context prompts to enhance interpretability.

### IV-B Tool-Based Methods

LLMs can augment their mathematical reasoning abilities by integrating
symbolic solvers and programming tools \[[111](#bib.bib111),
[112](#bib.bib112)\].

Single-tool methods focus on integrating LMs with a specific symbolic
reasoning framework. SymbLLM \[[45](#bib.bib45)\] enhances mathematical
word problem (MWP) solving by leveraging symbolic solvers, while
LeanReasoner \[[113](#bib.bib113)\] incorporates Lean theorem proving to
ensure logical consistency. PAL \[[43](#bib.bib43)\] improves
problem-solving by generating Python programs as intermediate reasoning
steps.

Multi-tool approaches extend LLM’s capabilities by enabling interactions
with multiple external tools. Toolformer \[[111](#bib.bib111)\] equips
LLMs with APIs for calculators, search engines, and symbolic computation
systems. ART \[[114](#bib.bib114)\] expands on this by integrating
automated retrieval-based tool selection, dynamically choosing the most
appropriate tool for each problem.

### IV-C Fundamental Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Methods

Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning enhances LLM’s performance in
mathematical tasks by decomposing problems into step-by-step logical
sequences \[[15](#bib.bib15)\].

Auto-CoT \[[41](#bib.bib41)\] automatically clusters representative
problems to generate effective reasoning examples, reducing reliance on
manual prompt engineering. Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RFT)
\[[72](#bib.bib72)\] refines solution paths by discarding incorrect
reasoning chains through rejection sampling. PromptPG-CoT
\[[40](#bib.bib40)\] optimizes CoT prompts using reinforcement learning
(RL), improving generalization across diverse mathematical tasks.

### IV-D Advanced Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Methods

Verify-based methods enhance mathematical reasoning by incorporating
self-verification mechanisms. VerifyCoT \[[115](#bib.bib115)\] ensures
deductive consistency, while SSC-CoT \[[116](#bib.bib116)\] validates
intermediate steps to prevent cascading reasoning errors.

Ensemble-based approaches improve accuracy by aggregating multiple
reasoning trajectories. Self-Consistency \[[37](#bib.bib37)\] selects
the most frequent solution among multiple sampled responses, while MCR
\[[117](#bib.bib117)\] uses majority voting across distinct reasoning
paths.

Planning-based methods organize reasoning hierarchically for structured
problem-solving. Tree-of-Thought (ToT) \[[118](#bib.bib118)\] structures
problem-solving as a tree-like process, exploring multiple reasoning
pathways. Graph-of-Thought (GoT) \[[119](#bib.bib119)\] extends this
concept to graph-based reasoning, capturing complex interdependencies
between logical steps.

Self-correction methods introduce iterative feedback loops to refine
responses. STaR \[[120](#bib.bib120)\] and CoRe \[[121](#bib.bib121)\]
employ Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to iteratively improve generated
solutions. OmegaPRM \[[122](#bib.bib122)\] applies automated supervision
to enhance Gemini’s mathematical reasoning capabilities.

Socratic Teaching Methods \[[123](#bib.bib123)\] refine mathematical
understanding through structured questioning. Socratic Models (SMs)
\[[124](#bib.bib124)\] integrate dialogue-based learning, enabling LLMs
to explore mathematical concepts interactively.

### IV-E Future Directions

While LLM-based mathematical reasoning has made remarkable progress,
several challenges remain:

- •
  Robust Verification: Ensuring LLMs generate provably correct
  mathematical solutions remains an open problem \[[125](#bib.bib125)\].
- •
  Handling Complex Proofs: Current models struggle with multi-step
  logical deductions in formal theorem proving \[[126](#bib.bib126)\].
- •
  Hybrid Symbolic-Neural Methods: Combining symbolic solvers with LLMs
  could enhance mathematical accuracy and reliability
  \[[127](#bib.bib127)\].

Future research should focus on improving reasoning consistency
\[[128](#bib.bib128)\], integrating interactive problem-solving
frameworks \[[129](#bib.bib129)\], and enhancing mathematical
comprehension through hybrid neural-symbolic approaches
\[[127](#bib.bib127)\].

= Multimodal

## V Comparison of Mathematical Large Language Models

Mathematical LLMs have evolved significantly as seen in benchmarks like
MATH \[[213](#bib.bib213)\] and geometry reasoning
\[[214](#bib.bib214)\], leveraging various architectural designs,
training methodologies, reasoning strategies, and tool integrations to
enhance mathematical problem-solving capabilities
\[[215](#bib.bib215)\].

However, open-source models lag behind proprietary ones like GPT-4
\[[216](#bib.bib216)\] and Gemini-Ultra \[[217](#bib.bib217)\]. To
bridge this gap, Deepseek \[[218](#bib.bib218)\] introduced a
domain-specific model (spmath) trained on the DeepSeekMath Corpus, a
high-quality dataset of 120B math tokens curated from Common Crawl using
a fastText-based classifier \[[219](#bib.bib219)\].

This model, initialized with DeepSeek-Coder-Base-v1.5 7B
\[[220](#bib.bib220)\], outperforms Minerva 540B \[[221](#bib.bib221)\]
on MATH \[[213](#bib.bib213)\] and exhibits strong multilingual
performance on CMATH \[[222](#bib.bib222)\] and AGIEval
\[[223](#bib.bib223)\]. Pre-training on code enhances mathematical
reasoning, supporting the idea that coding skills improve logic-based
problem-solving. spmath-Instruct 7B is fine-tuned using chain-of-thought
\[[224](#bib.bib224)\], program-of-thought \[[225](#bib.bib225),
[226](#bib.bib226)\], and tool-integrated reasoning
\[[227](#bib.bib227)\], achieving results comparable to 70B open-source
instruction-tuned models. Additionally, the Deepseek introduced Group
Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a RL method that eliminates the
critic model, significantly reducing training costs while improving
performance on GSM8K (82.9% $`\rightarrow`$ 88.2%) and MATH (46.8%
$`\rightarrow`$ 51.7%). A unified paradigm is presented in
\[[218](#bib.bib218)\] to connect various RL-based fine-tuning methods,
such as Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RFT)\[[72](#bib.bib72)\], Direct
Preference Optimization (DPO) \[[228](#bib.bib228)\], and Proximal
Policy Optimization (PPO) \[[229](#bib.bib229)\], with extensive
experiments exploring online vs. offline training, outcome vs. process
supervision, and single-turn vs. iterative RL. Deepseek also constructs
the DeepSeekMath Corpus using an iterative fastText-based classifier,
filtering 35.5M math-related web pages while preventing contamination
from evaluation datasets like GSM8K \[[230](#bib.bib230)\] and MATH
\[[213](#bib.bib213)\]. Evaluations show spmath-Base 7B surpasses
Mistral 7B \[[231](#bib.bib231)\] and Llemma 34B \[[232](#bib.bib232)\]
in mathematical reasoning, excelling in theorem proving on miniF2F
\[[233](#bib.bib233)\] and performing well in general reasoning (MMLU
\[[234](#bib.bib234)\], BBH \[[235](#bib.bib235)\]) and coding
(HumanEval \[[236](#bib.bib236)\], MBPP \[[237](#bib.bib237)\]). After
supervised fine-tuning with 776K mathematical instructions,
spmath-Instruct 7B outperforms all 7B models and rivals 70B
instruction-tuned models such as InternLM2-Math 20B, WizardMath 7B
\[[238](#bib.bib238)\], MetaMath 70B \[[239](#bib.bib239)\], and MAmmoTH
70B \[[240](#bib.bib240)\].

Table
[I](#S4.T1 "TABLE I ‣ IV-E Future Directions ‣ IV LLM-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
provides a comparative overview of multiple mathematical LLMs,
highlighting key aspects such as training methods, reasoning techniques,
RL applications, multi-modal capabilities, and accessibility. This
section explores the observed trends, major advancements, and existing
limitations in the domain of mathematical LLMs.

### V-A Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Reasoning

One of the most critical advancements in mathematical reasoning for LLMs
is the incorporation of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting
\[[15](#bib.bib15)\]. CoT enables models to break down complex problems
into intermediate steps, improving their ability to solve mathematical
and logical problems. Foundational CoT (F-CoT) is a common feature among
models such as MATH-PLM \[[94](#bib.bib94)\], ControlMath
\[[130](#bib.bib130)\], and EURUS \[[102](#bib.bib102)\], which helps
them generate structured multi-step solutions. More advanced forms of
CoT reasoning (A-CoT) are implemented in models like ToT
\[[118](#bib.bib118)\], Self-check \[[135](#bib.bib135)\], and Reflexion
\[[144](#bib.bib144)\], allowing them to refine their solutions
iteratively. Some recent models, such as QwQ \[[158](#bib.bib158)\], o1
\[[159](#bib.bib159)\], and LLaVA-o1 \[[162](#bib.bib162)\], employ Long
CoT, which extends reasoning over a longer context for more complex
mathematical problems.

### V-B Integration of External Tools

Tool integration is another key differentiator among mathematical LLMs.
Some models, such as Toolformer \[[111](#bib.bib111)\] and
LeanReasoner\[[113](#bib.bib113)\], incorporate external symbolic
solvers and calculators to enhance their accuracy. Other models, such as
Codex-math \[[44](#bib.bib44)\] and MuMath-Code \[[132](#bib.bib132)\],
rely on program-based reasoning, allowing them to execute mathematical
scripts and verify calculations dynamically. These tool-enhanced
approaches generally outperform models that rely purely on neural
network inference without external computation.

### V-C Training Paradigms: Pre-Training, Fine-Tuning, and Prompt Engineering

Mathematical LLMs adopt different training strategies, primarily
categorized into Pre-Training (Pre-T), Fine-Tuning (FT), and Prompt
Engineering. Pre-trained models, such as Minerva \[[96](#bib.bib96)\],
GPT-4o \[[160](#bib.bib160)\], and Gemini \[[163](#bib.bib163)\], are
trained on large mathematical datasets before being adapted for specific
problem-solving tasks. Fine-tuned models, such as WizardMath
\[[74](#bib.bib74)\] and InternLM-Math \[[108](#bib.bib108)\], undergo
domain-specific adaptations to improve their mathematical reasoning
skills. Prompt-based models, such as PAL \[[43](#bib.bib43)\] and PoT
\[[39](#bib.bib39)\], rely on structured prompt design to guide their
reasoning, making them more flexible for zero-shot and few-shot
learning.

### V-D RL for Mathematical Reasoning

RL techniques, particularly Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and
Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), have been applied to mathematical
LLMs to refine their solution generation process. Models like WizardMath
\[[74](#bib.bib74)\] and InternLM-Math \[[108](#bib.bib108)\] leverage
PPO to enhance their performance through iterative self-improvement.
Additionally, DPO-based models, such as EURUS \[[102](#bib.bib102)\] and
V-STaR \[[147](#bib.bib147)\], optimize their outputs based on user
preferences and ranking mechanisms. Recent advances, including Step-DPO
\[[154](#bib.bib154)\] and Flow-DPO \[[155](#bib.bib155)\], incorporate
structured planning into reinforcement learning to refine
problem-solving pathways.

### V-E Multi-Modal Capabilities

While most mathematical LLMs focus on text-based problem-solving, a
growing number of models support multi-modal reasoning, integrating text
and image inputs to solve visual mathematics problems. Notable
multi-modal models include GPT-4V \[[31](#bib.bib31)\], Gemini
\[[163](#bib.bib163)\], and GLM-4V \[[164](#bib.bib164)\], which
incorporate vision-language capabilities for tasks involving diagrams,
plots, and mathematical notation recognition. Similarly, Math-LLaVA
\[[166](#bib.bib166)\] and AtomThink \[[165](#bib.bib165)\] extend
multi-modal reasoning by fine-tuning on specialized mathematical
datasets. However, many models still lack this capability, indicating an
area for further improvement.

### V-F Open vs. Closed Access Models

The accessibility of mathematical LLMs varies significantly. Open-source
models such as EURUS \[[102](#bib.bib102)\], Qwen-Math
\[[107](#bib.bib107)\], and LINC \[[131](#bib.bib131)\] provide
transparency and adaptability, allowing researchers to fine-tune them
for specific applications. In contrast, proprietary models such as
GPT-4o \[[160](#bib.bib160)\], Gemini \[[163](#bib.bib163)\], and o1
\[[159](#bib.bib159)\] are closed-source, limiting external
modifications. The trade-off between openness and performance remains a
key consideration in model selection.

Mathematical LLMs continue to advance in reasoning capabilities, tool
integration, and training methodologies. While Chain-of-Thought
reasoning has become a standard technique for improving accuracy, models
differ in their use of external tools, reinforcement learning, and
multi-modal support. Additionally, open-source models provide
flexibility for customization, whereas closed-source models typically
offer superior performance at the cost of accessibility. Future
advancements are likely to focus on enhancing multi-modal mathematical
reasoning, better tool integrations, and more robust self-improving
learning mechanisms to achieve greater accuracy and efficiency in
solving complex mathematical problems.

## VI Performance Analysis of Mathematical Language Models

Mathematical LMs have demonstrated varying degrees of success across
different mathematical benchmarks. To assess the arithmetic and
mathematical capabilities of language models, various datasets and
evaluation methods have been proposed. Commonly used benchmarks include
MATH \[[94](#bib.bib94)\], GSM8K \[[142](#bib.bib142)\], and AGIEval
\[[192](#bib.bib192)\], which test models across different difficulty
levels and problem types. Traditional NLP evaluation metrics, such as
accuracy and perplexity, are insufficient for mathematical reasoning.
Instead, numeric predictions are evaluated using regression metrics such
as Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)
\[[241](#bib.bib241)\]. Adjusted Perplexity \[[60](#bib.bib60)\] has
also been proposed to account for numeric token distributions.

Some benchmarks adopt specialized evaluation strategies. The MATH
dataset \[[94](#bib.bib94)\] enforces strict answer formatting for
consistency, while AGIEval \[[192](#bib.bib192)\] employs Exact Match
and F1 scores for fill-in-the-blank questions. Recent research explores
automated assessment methods, including large language model-based
scoring \[[242](#bib.bib242)\], to provide more nuanced evaluations of
reasoning quality.

Given the complexity of mathematical problem-solving, developing
comprehensive evaluation metrics remains an open challenge. Future
directions should focus on refining benchmarks to assess multi-step
reasoning, logical consistency, and theorem-proving capabilities more
effectively.

Table [II](#S4.T2 "TABLE II ‣ IV-E Future Directions ‣ IV LLM-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
presents the performance of various text-based and multi-modal LMs on
multiple datasets, including MATH, GSM8K, MMLU, ASDiv, AIME, and others.

Among the text-based methods, Codex-math achieves the highest accuracy
on the MATH dataset (81.8%), while QwQ leads with a remarkable 90.6%.
For GSM8K, MCTSr outperforms other models with an accuracy of 96.6%,
followed closely by Diversity-of-Thought (96.3%) and Step-DPO (94.0%).
In the AIME dataset, Self-check achieves the highest performance with
81.2%. Similarly, in ASDiv, EURUS attains the best result (93.0%),
demonstrating its effectiveness in arithmetic reasoning tasks.

For dataset-specific performance, PoT leads in MathQA (89.1%), and CR
achieves the highest accuracy on the FOLIO dataset (98.0%). These
results highlight the strengths of specialized models in certain tasks.
Meanwhile, InternLM-Math and Qwen-Math show strong overall performance
across multiple datasets, particularly with Qwen-Math achieving 91.6% in
GSM8K and 82.8% in MMLU.

Multi-modal models, which leverage both textual and visual input,
generally perform better than their text-based counterparts. o1
outperforms all models on the MATH dataset with an accuracy of 94.8%,
and it also leads in MMLU (92.3%) and AIME (83.3%). GPT-4o also exhibits
strong performance in MMLU (88.0%), indicating the advantage of
integrating vision and language for mathematical reasoning. Qwen2-VL and
AtomThink show competitive results on multimodal datasets, with the
latter achieving the highest Math-V score (40.5%).

Overall, the results suggest that newer models incorporating diverse
reasoning strategies, self-consistency, and program-of-thought
approaches achieve higher accuracy. Future advancements in multi-modal
LMs are likely to further improve mathematical reasoning capabilities by
integrating structured symbolic processing with deep learning
architectures.

## VII Datasets

To train and evaluate the arithmetic and mathematical reasoning
abilities of language models, various math word problem (MWP) datasets
\[[191](#bib.bib191), [94](#bib.bib94), [142](#bib.bib142),
[7](#bib.bib7)\] have been developed. We categorize these datasets into
training, benchmark, and augmented datasets (Table
[III](#S4.T3 "TABLE III ‣ IV-E Future Directions ‣ IV LLM-Based Approaches for Mathematical Reasoning ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")).

### VII-A Training Datasets

Mathematical Calculation datasets extract numerical information from
text. Clinical Data \[[170](#bib.bib170)\] contains patient reports with
structured attributes, while Scientific Data \[[60](#bib.bib60)\]
includes research papers. DoQ \[[171](#bib.bib171)\] captures scalar
attribute values for over 350K nouns and verbs. VERBPHYSICS
\[[169](#bib.bib169)\] encodes action-object relations, and DROP
\[[58](#bib.bib58)\] tests discrete reasoning over adversarial
questions.

Math Word Problems datasets include AddSub \[[6](#bib.bib6)\], focusing
on addition and subtraction, and SingleOp \[[10](#bib.bib10)\], covering
multiplication and division. GSM8K \[[142](#bib.bib142)\] comprises
high-quality grade school problems requiring multi-step reasoning.
Math23k \[[5](#bib.bib5)\] and MATHPILE \[[178](#bib.bib178)\] provide
structured equations. To improve semantic parsing, Dolphin1878
\[[12](#bib.bib12)\] and SingleEq \[[13](#bib.bib13)\] enable equation
mapping. Recent datasets like HMWP \[[175](#bib.bib175)\] and MathQA
\[[180](#bib.bib180)\] expand problem diversity with multilingual and
multiple-choice questions.

Theorem Proving datasets such as Feit-Thompson \[[243](#bib.bib243)\]
and IsarStep \[[185](#bib.bib185)\] provide structured theorem proofs.
CoqGym \[[82](#bib.bib82)\] and LeanStep \[[95](#bib.bib95)\] integrate
proof tactics, while NaturalProofs \[[79](#bib.bib79)\] covers formal
and informal mathematics.

### VII-B Benchmark Datasets

Benchmark datasets evaluate mathematical reasoning. MAWPS
\[[188](#bib.bib188)\] and Dolphin18K \[[187](#bib.bib187)\] contain
diverse MWPs. MATH \[[94](#bib.bib94)\] provides competition-level
problems with step-by-step solutions. MGSM \[[190](#bib.bib190)\]
extends GSM8K into 10 languages. miniF2F \[[204](#bib.bib204)\]
benchmarks neural theorem proving across Lean, Isabelle, and HOL Light.
FOLIO \[[205](#bib.bib205)\] offers first-order logic reasoning
annotations.

Recent multimodal benchmarks assess vision-language models. MathVista
\[[194](#bib.bib194)\] evaluates diagram-based problems, while MMMU-Math
\[[201](#bib.bib201)\] and U-Math \[[203](#bib.bib203)\] focus on
university-level problem solving.

### VII-C Augmented Datasets

Augmented datasets enrich existing collections. Aggregate
\[[206](#bib.bib206)\] extends AllArith with additional perturbed
problems. MetaMathQA \[[73](#bib.bib73)\] reformulates mathematical
questions using GPT-4. Math5K \[[208](#bib.bib208)\] generates 50,000
new problems.

To enhance reasoning transparency, MathQA-Python \[[207](#bib.bib207)\]
converts text to code, and Lila \[[210](#bib.bib210)\] provides
Python-based solutions. PEN \[[211](#bib.bib211)\] annotates algebraic
explanations, while PRM800K \[[209](#bib.bib209)\] labels step-wise
mathematical reasoning correctness. MiniF2F+informal
\[[78](#bib.bib78)\] and NaturalProofs-Gen \[[212](#bib.bib212)\]
introduce informal theorem statements to theorem-proving datasets.

## VIII LLMs for Time Series Analysis: Challenges, Methods, and Future Directions

LLMs, such as GPT-4o and LLaMA \[[244](#bib.bib244)\], have achieved
remarkable success in natural language processing. Inspired by this,
researchers have started exploring their application in time series
analysis for tasks such as forecasting, classification, and anomaly
detection \[[245](#bib.bib245), [246](#bib.bib246)\]. By leveraging
their strong pattern recognition capabilities, LLMs have been integrated
into various domains where time series data play a crucial role. These
applications span across finance \[[247](#bib.bib247),
[248](#bib.bib248)\], healthcare \[[249](#bib.bib249),
[250](#bib.bib250)\], traffic forecasting \[[251](#bib.bib251)\], and
mobility prediction \[[252](#bib.bib252)\]. In finance, LLMs assist in
stock market forecasting by analyzing textual and numerical data. In
healthcare, LLMs are leveraged for Electrocardiogram classification and
electronic health record analysis \[[249](#bib.bib249)\].

However, applying LLMs to time series data presents unique challenges:
(1) diverse formats and domains make model training complex, and (2)
real-world time series data often exhibit non-stationary properties,
leading to concept drift that impedes continuous
adaptation \[[253](#bib.bib253)\].

To address these challenges, recent works exploit pre-trained LLMs to
analyze time series data without training new models from scratch. This
section systematically categorizes these efforts into five key
strategies: (1) direct querying of LLMs, (2) tokenization techniques for
numerical data, (3) prompt engineering, (4) fine-tuning methods, and (5)
integration of LLMs with existing time series models. Additionally, we
discuss domain-specific applications and propose future research
directions.

### VIII-A Taxonomy of LLMs in Time Series Analysis

General Pipeline. LLM-based time series analysis can be performed
through three main approaches: direct querying, fine-tuning, or
integrating LLMs into models. Fine-tuning involves tokenizing time
series data, enhancing representation with prompts, and adapting LLMs
for downstream tasks.

Direct Query. Works like PromptCast \[[254](#bib.bib254)\] and
LLMTime \[[255](#bib.bib255)\] use zero-shot or few-shot learning by
querying LLMs with structured prompts.

Tokenization. Patching techniques \[[256](#bib.bib256),
[257](#bib.bib257)\] and STL decomposition \[[258](#bib.bib258)\] help
convert numerical time series data into a format interpretable by LLMs.

Prompt Design. Context-enhanced and soft prompts \[[259](#bib.bib259),
[260](#bib.bib260)\] improve the ability of LLMs to process time series
data effectively.

Fine-tuning. Techniques such as LoRA \[[261](#bib.bib261)\] help
optimize specific layers of LLMs to adapt them to time series
tasks \[[257](#bib.bib257), [256](#bib.bib256)\].

Model Integration. Some methods use LLMs as feature enhancement tools in
multimodal frameworks \[[262](#bib.bib262), [263](#bib.bib263),
[249](#bib.bib249)\].

Open challenges include:

- •
  Improved tokenization and prompts to better represent time series
  patterns \[[264](#bib.bib264)\].
- •
  Interpretability via prototype-based or gradient-based
  methods \[[265](#bib.bib265)\].
- •
  Multi-modality fusion techniques for integrating time series with
  text, images, and tabular data.
- •
  Domain generalization techniques for robust performance across diverse
  datasets.
- •
  Scaling laws to understand how model size affects time series
  performance.
- •
  Time series LLM agents for decision-making applications.
- •
  Bias and safety mechanisms to ensure reliability in critical domains.

TABLE IV: Taxonomy of LLM-based time series methods. The data type TS
denotes standard time series, STS represents spatiotemporal time series,
and the prefix MM- indicates multimodal inputs. The task entry Multiple
includes forecasting, classification, imputation, and anomaly detection.
Query refers to direct LLM-based querying, Token indicates time series
tokenization design, Prompt denotes the use of textual or parameterized
prompts, Tune represents fine-tuning of LLM parameters, and Fusion
specifies whether LLMs are integrated into external models for
downstream tasks.

## IX Comparison of commonly used LLMs based on their technical specifications

LLMs have evolved significantly, with various models offering different
strengths and weaknesses. In this section we compare some famous LLMs
including GPT-4 Turbo, LLaMA3, DeepSeek-R1, Mixtral, Claude3, and
Gemini1.5. These models differ in terms of architecture, training data,
computational efficiency, response accuracy, and suitability for
specific applications. Some models prioritize speed and
cost-effectiveness, while others focus on reasoning capabilities,
multilingual support, or specialized domain knowledge.

### IX-A GPT-4 Turbo (OpenAI)

Pros:

- •
  Best overall reasoning and language understanding.
- •
  Supports multi-modal input (text and images).
- •
  Handles up to 128K tokens³³ 3 100 tokens are about 60-80 English words
  \[[268](#bib.bib268)\].
- •
  Strong API ecosystem and enterprise adoption.

Cons:

- •
  Closed-source, making fine-tuning difficult.
- •
  Expensive compared to open-source alternatives.
- •
  Some guardrails limit responses to certain queries.

### IX-B LLaMA 2 & 3 (Meta)

Pros:

- •
  Open-source, allowing fine-tuning and self-hosting.
- •
  Efficient performance and competitive results.
- •
  Strong research community support.

Cons:

- •
  Weaker reasoning than GPT-4 Turbo.
- •
  Shorter context windows (4K–32K tokens) \[[269](#bib.bib269)\].
- •
  Requires high-end GPUs for full deployment.

### IX-C DeepSeek-R1 (DeepSeek)

Pros:

- •
  Cost-Effective Development: Achieved performance comparable to GPT-4
  at a fraction of the cost.
- •
  Efficient Resource Utilization: Trained using about 2,048 NVIDIA H800
  GPUs, significantly fewer than competitors \[[270](#bib.bib270)\].
- •
  Open-Source Commitment: Models are freely available under the MIT
  license, promoting transparency and customization.
- •
  Rapid Adoption: Quickly became the most downloaded application on the
  social media, surpassing competitors like ChatGPT.

Cons:

- •
  Limited Language Support: Primarily optimized for English and Chinese,
  with less proficiency in other languages.
- •
  Less Established Ecosystem: Being relatively new, it lacks the
  extensive integration and community support seen with models like
  GPT-4.

Best for: Users and developers seeking a cost-effective, open-source
alternative with strong performance in English and Chinese.

### IX-D Mixtral (Mistral AI)

Pros:

- •
  Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture for efficiency.
- •
  Open-source and highly optimized.
- •
  Compact and fast inference speed.

Cons:

- •
  Shorter context length than GPT-4 (4K-32K tokens)
  \[[271](#bib.bib271)\].
- •
  Less adoption in enterprise applications.

### IX-E Claude 3 (Anthropic)

Pros:

- •
  Best alignment and ethical AI practices.
- •
  Handles very long documents (up to 200K tokens
  \[[272](#bib.bib272)\]).
- •
  More human-like responses and high creativity.

Cons:

- •
  Slower response times compared to GPT-4 Turbo.
- •
  Closed-source, requiring API access.

### IX-F Gemini 1.5 Pro (Google)

Pros:

- •
  Strong multi-modal capabilities (text, images, and audio).
- •
  Deep integration with Google services.
- •
  Very long context windows (up to 2M tokens \[[273](#bib.bib273)\]).

Cons:

- •
  Still catching up to OpenAI in benchmarks.
- •
  Limited open-source versions.

Table
[V](#S9.T5 "TABLE V ‣ IX-F Gemini 1.5 Pro (Google) ‣ IX Comparison of commonly used LLMs based on their technical specifications ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
summarizes the technical specifications of commonly used LLMs based on
context length, strength, and weaknesses.

| Model          | Open-Source | Context Length | Strengths                       | Weaknesses               |
| -------------- | ----------- | -------------- | ------------------------------- | ------------------------ |
| GPT-4 Turbo    | ✗           | 128K           | Best reasoning, multi-modal     | Expensive, closed-source |
| LLaMA 2/3      | ✓           | 4K–32K         | Open-source, efficient          | Weaker reasoning         |
| DeepSeek-R1    | ✓           | 12K            | Good multilingual support       | Less adoption            |
| Mixtral        | ✓           | 4K–32K         | Efficient, compact              | Shorter context          |
| Claude 3       | ✗           | 200K           | Ethical AI, document processing | Slower responses         |
| Gemini 1.5 Pro | ✗           | 2M             | Multi-modal, long context       | Not fully open-source    |
