specifications

## X Application of LLms on optimization and control

LLMs in solving control and optimization problems is essential for
understanding their strengths, limitations, and potential applications.
As these models continue to advance, their capabilities in multi-step
reasoning, broad knowledge retrieval, and intuitive code generation make
them promising tools for tackling complex computational tasks. However,
their limitations—such as logical inconsistencies, sensitivity to prompt
variations, and susceptibility to errors—necessitate a rigorous
assessment of their reliability in control and optimization scenarios.

A systematic evaluation of LLMs in these domains allows us to determine
the extent to which they can autonomously handle problem-solving, adapt
to dynamically changing parameters, and refine solutions iteratively.
Moreover, this investigation provides critical insights into how LLMs
can complement traditional solvers, optimizing human-AI workflows and
enhancing the safety, robustness, and efficiency of solutions in
real-world applications. Additionally, by testing these models in
structured optimization problems, we may uncover novel solution
strategies that lead to improved computational efficiency and innovation
in control systems.

To address this objective, we systematically test LLMs across a range of
control and optimization tasks, including:

\(i\) Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) for Battery Energy Storage
Systems (BESS): Optimizing charge/discharge scheduling while minimizing
operational costs and adhering to system constraints.

\(ii\) Linear Quadratic Control (LQC) for water level regulation in
interconnected tanks: Evaluating the ability of LLMs to design and
analyze optimal control strategies for fluid management.

\(iii\) Climate dataset analysis: Utilizing LLMs for pattern
recognition, forecasting, and decision-making based on historical
climate data.

\(iv\) Multi-Agent Linear Programming Optimization: Exploring how LLMs
can assist in coordinating multiple decision-making agents to achieve
optimal solutions.

By rigorously testing LLMs in these diverse applications, we aim to
identify their strengths and weaknesses in solving complex mathematical
optimization and control problems. This research will inform best
practices for integrating LLMs into existing computational workflows,
ultimately advancing the field of AI-driven optimization and control.

##### Code availability statement

The Python scripts that we use for each example leverage multiple
libraries to perform data processing, optimization, and AI-driven
analysis. They utilize NumPy \[[274](#bib.bib274)\] and Pandas
\[[275](#bib.bib275)\] for numerical and tabular data manipulation,
while Matplotlib \[[276](#bib.bib276)\] is used for visualization. Scipy
\[[275](#bib.bib275)\] provides numerical optimization functions,
particularly for linear programming, and Scikit-learn
\[[277](#bib.bib277)\] is employed for preprocessing tasks like scaling.
For geospatial analysis, Cartopy \[[278](#bib.bib278)\] is used to map
wind data. The asyncio \[[279](#bib.bib279)\] library enables
asynchronous execution, ensuring smooth parallel processing, especially
in federated optimization scenarios. The script also incorporates
OpenAI’s API to generate insights from wind and battery storage data,
assisting in decision-making. Furthermore, PuLP \[[280](#bib.bib280)\]
is used for mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) to optimize battery
charge/discharge schedules dynamically. Finally, AutoGen
\[[281](#bib.bib281)\] is used to create multi-agent frameworks for
executing linear programming problems asynchronously. In Appendix
[-A](#A0.SS1 "-A Code Availability Statement ‣ Acknowledgment ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
we provide the link to the repository containing the codes that are used
for the simulation.

### X-A An example of the LLm application on the BESS

This section details an optimization problem for the operation of a BESS
with LLMs. Our objective is to minimize the net operational cost (or
maximize net revenue) by scheduling the charging and discharging of a
battery over a discrete 10-hour planning horizon. We incorporate fuzzy
logic rules to capture realistic decision-making heuristics concerning
electricity prices, battery state of charge (SOC), and operational
switching considerations.

#### X-A1 System Overview

We consider a BESS with the following characteristics:

- •
  Battery capacity: $`SOC_{\max}=100\ \text{kWh}`$
- •
  Initial SOC: $`SOC_{0}=50\ \text{kWh}`$
- •
  Charging efficiency: $`\eta=0.90`$
- •
  Discharging efficiency: $`\frac{1}{\eta}=0.90`$
- •
  Max charge/discharge power:
  $`P_{\text{charge}}^{\max}=P_{\text{discharge}}^{\max}=20\ \text{kW}`$

We operate over time steps $`t=1,\dots,10`$, each representing a
one-hour interval.

#### X-A2 Decision Variables

- •
  $`P_{\text{charge}}(t)\ [\text{kW}]`$: Charging power during hour
  $`t`$.
- •
  $`P_{\text{discharge}}(t)\ [\text{kW}]`$: Discharging power during
  hour $`t`$.
- •
  $`SOC(t)\ [\text{kWh}]`$: State of charge of the battery at time
  $`t`$.

#### X-A3 Parameters

#### Electricity Prices and Revenues

Let

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
C=\bigl[\,20,\,25,\,15,\,10,\,30,\,35,\,40,\,20,\,15,\,10\bigr]\quad\text{(\$/kWh)},
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R=\bigl[\,30,\,35,\,25,\,20,\,40,\,45,\,50,\,30,\,25,\,20\bigr]\quad\text{(\$/kWh)}.
``` |  |

Here, $`C_{t}`$ is the cost of purchasing electricity in hour $`t`$, and
$`R_{t}`$ is the revenue rate from selling electricity in hour $`t`$.

### X-B Constraints

##### Battery Capacity Bound

|     |                                                   |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       0\;\leq\;SOC(t)\;\leq\;SOC_{\max},\quad\forall t.
       ```                                                |     |

##### Power Limits

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
0\;\leq\;P_{\text{charge}}(t)\;\leq\;P_{\text{charge}}^{\max}=20,\quad\forall t,
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
0\;\leq\;P_{\text{discharge}}(t)\;\leq\;P_{\text{discharge}}^{\max}=20,\quad\forall t.
``` |  |

##### Energy Balance

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
SOC(t+1)=SOC(t)\;+\;\eta\cdot P_{\text{charge}}(t)\\
\;-\;\frac{1}{\eta}\cdot P_{\text{discharge}}(t),\quad t=1,\ldots,9.
``` |  | (1) |

We take $`SOC(1)=SOC_{0}=50\,\text{kWh}`$ as the initial condition.

##### No Simultaneous Charge/Discharge

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P_{\text{charge}}(t)\,\cdot\,P_{\text{discharge}}(t)\;=\;0,\quad\forall t.
``` |  |

This ensures the battery cannot charge and discharge in the same hour.

### X-C Objective Function

We wish to minimize the total net cost $`Z`$ over the 10-hour horizon:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min Z\;=\;\sum_{t=1}^{10}\Bigl(C_{t}\cdot P_{\text{charge}}(t)\;-\;R_{t}\cdot P_{\text{discharge}}(t)\Bigr).
``` |  |

The term $`C_{t}\,P_{\text{charge}}(t)`$ represents the cost of
charging, while $`-\,R_{t}\,P_{\text{discharge}}(t)`$ represents revenue
gained from discharging.

#### X-C1 Fuzzy Logic Integration

We enrich the classical optimization with fuzzy logic rules to guide or
penalize decisions that deviate from human-like strategies. The fuzzy
rules help shape how aggressively the system charges or discharges based
on electricity prices, battery SOC, and the desire to avoid frequent
switching.

1.  1.
    If the electricity price $`C_{t}`$ is high, prioritize discharging.
2.  2.
    If the battery SOC is below $`30\%`$ of $`SOC_{\max}`$, limit
    discharging and prioritize charging.
3.  3.
    If $`C_{t}`$ is low, delay or reduce charging unless SOC is
    critically low.
4.  4.
    Avoid frequent switching between charging and discharging.

In Table
[VI](#A0.T6 "TABLE VI ‣ -B Prompt for BESS to GPT4O ‣ Acknowledgment ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
(Appendix) we have shown an example of how an hour-by-hour schedule
might look based on the output of the LLM. Actual solutions may vary
depending on specific fuzzy membership functions, penalty weights, and
solver nuances. In addition, the LLM model is also plays an important
role for the response to the prompt. In this example we used GPT4O LLM
model.

##### Cost Computation Example

The total cost $`Z`$ is computed by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Z=\sum_{t=1}^{10}\Bigl(C_{t}\cdot P_{\text{charge}}(t)\;-\;R_{t}\cdot P_{\text{discharge}}(t)\Bigr).
``` |  |

If, for instance, at hour $`2`$ we have $`P_{\text{charge}}(2)=0`$ and
$`P_{\text{discharge}}(2)=10`$, then the hour-$`2`$ cost contribution is

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \text{Cost}_{2}=25\times 0\;-\;35\times 10\;=\;-350\;\$,
       ```                                                       |     |

indicating a net profit of \$350 at hour 2 (negative cost).

### X-D LLM-Driven Fuzzy Logic Control for Water Level Regulation in Interconnected Tanks

Water level regulation in interconnected tank systems is a fundamental
problem in control engineering, frequently addressed using classical and
modern control techniques such as Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID)
controllers, Model Predictive Control (MPC), or fuzzy logic controllers.

In this section, we integrate a LLM as a decision-making component in a
fuzzy logic-based control system to optimize the regulation of water
levels while minimizing a quadratic cost function. This novel approach
leverages natural language processing (NLP)-based reasoning within an
LLM to generate real-time control decisions based on predefined fuzzy
logic rules.

#### X-D1 System Model

The dynamics of the interconnected tanks are represented using a
state-space formulation, where the water levels in the two tanks form
the system state:

|     |                                         |     |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathbf{x}(t)=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}(t)\\
       x_{2}(t)\end{bmatrix},
       ```                                      |     | (2) |

The system evolves according to the linear state-space model:

|     |                                                             |     |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \dot{\mathbf{x}}(t)=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}(t)+\mathbf{B}u(t),
       ```                                                          |     | (3) |

where:

|     |                                                             |     |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}-0.1&0.05\\
       0.05&-0.1\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathbf{B}=\begin{bmatrix}0.1\\
       0\end{bmatrix},
       ```                                                          |     | (4) |

The control input $`u(t)`$ represents the pump flow rate, which is
constrained within the range $`[-u_{\max},u_{\max}]`$, with
$`u_{\max}=0.5`$. The cost function to be minimized is a quadratic
penalty function:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J=Q_{1}(x_{1}-x_{1,\text{desired}})^{2}+Q_{2}(x_{2}-x_{2,\text{desired}})^{2}+Ru^{2},
``` |  | (5) |

where:

- •
  $`Q_{1}=10`$, $`Q_{2}=8`$ are state penalty weights,
- •
  $`R=2`$ penalizes excessive control effort,
- •
  $`x_{1,\text{desired}}=2.0`$, $`x_{2,\text{desired}}=1.5`$ are the
  target levels.

#### X-D2 Fuzzy Logic-Based Control Using LLM

In this scenario the LLM is integrated as an inference engine that
follows predefined fuzzy logic rules to determine the control action
$`u(t)`$ at each time step. The control strategy involves:

1.  1.
    Assessing Deviation: The LLM compares the actual water levels
    $`x_{1},x_{2}`$ with their desired levels.
2.  2.
    Applying Fuzzy Rules:

    - •
      If $`x_{1}>x_{1,\text{desired}}`$, decrease $`u`$.
    - •
      If $`x_{2}<x_{2,\text{desired}}`$, increase $`u`$.
    - •
      Balance the adjustments to avoid oscillations.
3.  3.
    Minimizing the Cost Function: The LLM aims to select $`u(t)`$ that
    optimally reduces deviations while considering energy efficiency.

At each time step $`t`$, a prompt (see Algorithm
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ X-D2 Fuzzy Logic-Based Control Using LLM ‣ X-D LLM-Driven Fuzzy Logic Control for Water Level Regulation in Interconnected Tanks ‣ X Application of LLms on optimization and control ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models"))
is dynamically generated and sent to the LLM (see appendix
[-C](#A0.SS3 "-C Prompt for Tank Level Control ‣ Acknowledgment ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")).
In Algorithm
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ X-D2 Fuzzy Logic-Based Control Using LLM ‣ X-D LLM-Driven Fuzzy Logic Control for Water Level Regulation in Interconnected Tanks ‣ X Application of LLms on optimization and control ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
we present a general procedure that is used for tank level control.

The LLM generates a response, which is parsed to extract the control
input. If parsing fails, a fallback $`u=0`$ is used.

1: Input: Initial state $`\mathbf{x}_{0}=[x_{1},x_{2}]^{\top}`$, desired
state
$`\mathbf{x}_{d}=[x_{1,\text{desired}},x_{2,\text{desired}}]^{\top}`$

2: Parameters:

3:  System matrices $`\mathbf{A}`$, $`\mathbf{B}`$

4:  Quadratic cost weights $`Q_{1},Q_{2},R`$

5:  Maximum pump flow rate $`u_{\max}`$

6:  Time step $`\Delta t`$, total time $`T`$

7: Initialize: Set $`t=0`$, state $`\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{0}`$, control
input $`u=0`$

8: while $`t<T`$ do

9:   Construct *prompt* with current state and system parameters

10:   Send prompt to LLM to obtain fuzzy logic-based control input
$`u(t)`$

11:   Parse the LLM response to extract $`u(t)`$

12:   Ensure control input is within bounds:

|     |                                                    |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       u(t)\leftarrow\text{clip}(u(t),-u_{\max},u_{\max})
       ```                                                 |     |

13:   Compute state derivative:

|     |                                                   |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \dot{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{B}u
       ```                                                |     |

14:   Update system state:

|     |                                                              |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathbf{x}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}+\dot{\mathbf{x}}\cdot\Delta t
       ```                                                           |     |

15:   $`(t,x_{1},x_{2},u)`$ for analysis

16:   Increment time: $`t\leftarrow t+\Delta t`$

17: end while

18: Output: Time trajectory of states and control inputs

Algorithm 1 LLM-Based Fuzzy Control for Interconnected Tanks

The model is simulated over a 10-second horizon, with the LLM making
decisions at each step. The summary of the simulation results produced
by LLM is shown in Table
[VII](#A0.T7 "TABLE VII ‣ -C Prompt for Tank Level Control ‣ Acknowledgment ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models").

### X-E Application of Large Language Models in Time Series dataset analysis

In this section we consider the application of LLMs for Wind and
Pressure Data Analysis for renewable energies. We describe the
implementation of a climate data analysis framework integrating machine
learning techniques for wind speed and pressure assessment in Germany
\[[282](#bib.bib282)\]. The system processes CMIP6 climate datasets,
extracts and interpolates meteorological variables, computes statistical
features, and formulates queries for a LLM to analyze patterns and
trends. Here we detail the algorithmic structure and highlights how LLMs
assist in interpreting climate data for renewable energy applications.
Wind energy forecasting and optimal wind turbine placement require an
extensive analysis of historical meteorological data. Traditional
statistical methods provide limited insights due to their inability to
capture complex interactions among different environmental factors. The
integration of advanced machine learning techniques, specifically LLMs,
offers a promising approach to identifying patterns, anomalies, and
correlations in meteorological datasets. The objective here is to: (i)
Extract and preprocess wind speed and pressure data from climate
datasets. (ii) Compute and analyze statistical features across multiple
geographic locations. (iii) Utilize LLMs to generate domain-specific
insights for optimal wind turbine placement and energy forecasting. (iv)
Assess the effectiveness of LLMs in climate data interpretation and
decision-making.

Algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ X-E Application of Large Language Models in Time Series dataset analysis ‣ X Application of LLms on optimization and control ‣ A Survey on Mathematical Reasoning and Optimization with Large Language Models")
outlines the step-by-step procedure for climate data processing and
LLM-assisted analysis.

Input: Time series dataset, e.g. CMIP6 climate including wind and
pressure datasets, real wind power production

Output: LLM-generated insights for energy forecasting

Step 1: Data Acquisition

Extract pressure data and wind speed from CMIP6 dataset.

Step 2: Data Preprocessing and Interpolation

Interpolate wind speed

Interpolate pressure

Normalize data using

Step 3: Statistical Feature Extraction

Compute location-wise statistics using such as mean, variance, skewness,
kurtosis, etc.

Step 4: LLM Integration for Data Interpretation

Formulate prompt incorporating computed statistics.

Send prompt to LLM model.

Retrieve and process LLM-generated insights.

Step 5: Interpretation and Decision Support

Analyze LLM response for wind energy site selection.

Identify temporal patterns for optimal energy capture.

Suggest additional data transformations for improved accuracy.

Output: LLM response based on the Prompt

Algorithm 2 Climate dataset Analysis using LLMs

In this scenario the LLM serves as an intelligent assistant, performing
the following tasks: (i) Pattern Recognition: Identifies anomalies,
trends, and correlations in the data. (ii) Data Interpretation: Converts
numerical statistics into actionable insights. (iii) Domain-Specific
Suggestions: Offers recommendations for wind farm placement and data
augmentation strategies. (iv) Exploratory Analysis: Proposes hypotheses
for further study based on statistical distributions.

The advantages are: (i) Automates statistical analysis and
interpretation. (ii) Reduces manual effort in data exploration. (iii)
Provides contextual recommendations for renewable energy planning.
Moreover, challenges are: (i) LLM-generated insights must be validated
against domain expertise. (ii) Computational overhead due to large-scale
data processing. (iii) Dependency on high-quality input prompts for
effective responses.

### X-F Application of Multi-Agent LLms to solve Linear Programming

Optimization problems, particularly Linear Programming (LP), play a
crucial role in logistics, resource allocation, and decision-making.
Traditional LP solvers are effective but operate in a static manner
without real-time constraint validation or iterative execution based on
external feedback. This section presents an AutoGen Multi-Agent LP
Optimization framework, which utilizes LLMs to autonomously verify
constraints and execute optimization procedures.

AutoGen is an open-source Python framework designed to streamline the
development of applications utilizing LLMs through the collaboration of
multiple agents. These agents can communicate with each other,
incorporate human inputs, and employ external tools to collectively
tackle complex tasks \[[283](#bib.bib283)\]. Developed by Microsoft
Research, AutoGen offers customizable agents capable of conversing,
executing code, and integrating various functionalities, thereby
simplifying the orchestration and automation of sophisticated LLM
workflows. Its flexible architecture supports diverse applications,
including code generation, debugging, and problem-solving across various
domains \[[281](#bib.bib281)\].

The system consists of an EvaluatorAgent for constraint validation and
an LPExecutorAgent for executing the solver iteratively. The proposed
approach introduces an intelligent layer of decision-making, improving
the reliability and adaptability of LP-based optimization.

### X-G Application to a Single Linear Programming Problem

#### X-G1 Problem Statement

The optimization task is a transportation cost minimization problem.
Given two warehouses supplying goods to three stores, the objective is
to determine the optimal transportation allocation that minimizes costs
while satisfying supply and demand constraints.

#### X-G2 Mathematical Formulation

The LP model is defined as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{Minimize }Z`$ | $`\displaystyle=4x_{1}+6x_{2}+9x_{3}+5x_{4}+3x_{5}+8x_{6},`$ |  | (6) |

Subject to:

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 50`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{(Supply limit at Warehouse 1)},`$ |  | (7) |
|  | $`\displaystyle x_{4}+x_{5}+x_{6}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 60`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{(Supply limit at Warehouse 2)},`$ |  | (8) |
|  | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{4}`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq 30`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{(Demand at Store 1)},`$ |  | (9) |
|  | $`\displaystyle x_{2}+x_{5}`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq 40`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{(Demand at Store 2)},`$ |  | (10) |
|  | $`\displaystyle x_{3}+x_{6}`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq 20`$ | $`\displaystyle\text{(Demand at Store 3)},`$ |  | (11) |
|  | $`\displaystyle x_{i}`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq 0`$ | $`\displaystyle\forall i\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}.`$ |  | (12) |

### X-H Application to a Multi-Stage LP Optimization Problem

#### X-H1 Problem Statement

In a more complex scenario, the optimization problem involves three
separate but interconnected LP problems where solutions from the first
stage influence constraints in the subsequent stages. This is relevant
for dynamic supply chain optimization, multi-time-period scheduling, and
adaptive cost minimization.

### X-I Mathematical Formulation

Each LP problem is formulated similarly to the single problem but with
additional constraints:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{Minimize }Z_{1}`$ | $`\displaystyle=3x_{1}+7x_{2}+2x_{3}+6x_{4}+4x_{5}+5x_{6},`$ |  | (13) |

Subject to:

|     |                                     |                           |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------|---------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 40,`$ |     | (14) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{4}+x_{5}+x_{6}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 70,`$ |     | (15) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{4}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 20,`$ |     | (16) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{2}+x_{5}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 30,`$ |     | (17) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{3}+x_{6}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 25,`$ |     | (18) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{i}`$             | $`\displaystyle\geq 0,`$  |     | (19) |

For the second and third stage problems:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{Minimize }Z_{2}`$ | $`\displaystyle=5x_{1}+4x_{2}+6x_{3}+3x_{4}+7x_{5}+8x_{6},`$ |  | (20) |

Subject to:

|     |                                     |                           |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------|---------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 60,`$ |     | (21) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{4}+x_{5}+x_{6}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 80,`$ |     | (22) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{4}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 35,`$ |     | (23) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{2}+x_{5}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 25,`$ |     | (24) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{3}+x_{6}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 30,`$ |     | (25) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{i}`$             | $`\displaystyle\geq 0,`$  |     | (26) |

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{Minimize }Z_{3}`$ | $`\displaystyle=6x_{1}+8x_{2}+7x_{3}+5x_{4}+9x_{5}+4x_{6},`$ |  | (27) |

Subject to:

|     |                                     |                           |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------|---------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 50,`$ |     | (28) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{4}+x_{5}+x_{6}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq 75,`$ |     | (29) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{4}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 28,`$ |     | (30) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{2}+x_{5}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 38,`$ |     | (31) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{3}+x_{6}`$       | $`\displaystyle\geq 22,`$ |     | (32) |
|     | $`\displaystyle x_{i}`$             | $`\displaystyle\geq 0,`$  |     | (33) |

#### X-I1 Role of LLMs

To integrate intelligent decision-making in LP optimization, we employ
LLM-based agents using the AutoGen framework \[[281](#bib.bib281)\]. The
system consists of two key agents: (i) EvaluatorAgent, (ii)
LPExecutorAgent.

### X-J EvaluatorAgent: Constraint Validation

Before executing the LP solver, an agent verifies whether constraints
are correctly defined. This prevents unnecessary computation and ensures
the solver is executed under valid conditions.

#### X-J1 System Message for EvaluatorAgent

``` ltx_verbatim
ΨΨ$system_message$: "You verify if the
        LP constraints are satisfied
        before execution."
Ψ
````

#### X-J2 Prompt used by EvaluatorAgent

```ltx_verbatim
ΨΨ"validate constraints."
Ψ
```

The agent responds with:

- •
  ✓Constraints are valid. Proceed with LP execution.
- •
  ✗Invalid constraints detected. Execution halted.

#### X-J3 LPExecutorAgent: Optimization Execution

Upon receiving confirmation that constraints are valid, the execution
agent runs the LP solver three times for consistency.

##### System Message for LPExecutorAgent

```ltx_verbatim
ΨΨ$system_message$: "You execute the LP solver
        only if constraints are valid."
Ψ
```

##### Prompt used by LPExecutorAgent

```ltx_verbatim
ΨΨ"Proceed with LP execution."
Ψ
```

The agent iteratively solves the LP and returns solutions for analysis.

#### X-J4 Implementation and Execution

The process follows these steps:

1.  1.  The EvaluatorAgent checks the validity of constraints.
2.  2.  If constraints are valid, the LPExecutorAgent executes the LP solver
        three times.
3.  3.  The results are compiled and analyzed for consistency.

#### X-J5 Results and Discussion

By leveraging LLMs for constraint validation and execution, the system
prevents errors due to incorrect LP formulations and ensures a
structured execution process. The use of multiple runs improves solution
robustness and reliability. Future extensions of this framework may
include:

- •
  Adaptive learning mechanisms to refine constraints dynamically.
- •
  Multi-objective optimization for real-world applications.
- •
  Integration with reinforcement learning for decision-making in dynamic
  environments.

## XI Challenges and Further Directions

Despite the remarkable advancements in mathematical language models,
several critical challenges remain unresolved. These challenges include
faithfulness, multi-modal integration, uncertainty, evaluation, creative
mathematical reasoning, application in education, and data scarcity.

##### Faithfulness

Mathematical LLMs suffer from hallucinations, generating incorrect or
misleading results that undermine reliability \[[284](#bib.bib284),
[84](#bib.bib84)\]. Several approaches attempt to mitigate this issue by
incorporating additional knowledge \[[285](#bib.bib285)\], reinforcement
learning from human feedback \[[209](#bib.bib209)\], tool usage
\[[111](#bib.bib111), [112](#bib.bib112)\], and verification mechanisms
\[[286](#bib.bib286), [115](#bib.bib115), [287](#bib.bib287),
[288](#bib.bib288), [285](#bib.bib285), [289](#bib.bib289)\]. However,
these improvements remain limited, necessitating further research to
enhance trustworthiness and practical applicability.

##### Multi-Modal Integration

Mathematical problems often require multi-modal reasoning, involving not
just text but also diagrams, graphs, and equations \[[290](#bib.bib290),
[291](#bib.bib291)\]. While existing LLMs perform well in text-based
tasks, they struggle with processing and integrating multi-modal
information \[[28](#bib.bib28), [291](#bib.bib291)\]. Compared to
general vision-language tasks like VQA \[[292](#bib.bib292)\] and image
captioning \[[293](#bib.bib293)\], mathematical multi-modal reasoning is
significantly more complex. Advancements in this area are essential for
improving problem-solving capabilities in geometry and symbolic
reasoning.

##### Uncertainty

LLMs inherently produce ambiguous outputs, particularly in probabilistic
reasoning and problems with incomplete information \[[294](#bib.bib294),
[295](#bib.bib295)\]. While deterministic calculations are well-handled,
tasks requiring fuzzy logic, approximate solutions, or uncertain
constraints remain challenging. Some studies address this through
controlled generation \[[296](#bib.bib296)\], but a more robust approach
is needed to ensure mathematical models accurately navigate uncertainty.

##### Evaluation

Existing evaluation methods struggle to comprehensively assess
mathematical reasoning capabilities. Traditional NLP metrics fail to
capture the logical depth required for complex problem-solving. Several
benchmarks have been proposed \[[191](#bib.bib191), [94](#bib.bib94),
[142](#bib.bib142), [7](#bib.bib7)\], alongside evaluation metrics
\[[297](#bib.bib297), [298](#bib.bib298), [299](#bib.bib299),
[66](#bib.bib66)\]. However, ensuring a fair assessment across
arithmetic, theorem proving, and applied mathematics remains an ongoing
challenge.

##### Creative Mathematical Reasoning

While LLMs excel at solving existing problems, their ability to
formulate and prove new mathematical theorems remains limited.
Mathematical creativity requires deep conceptual understanding beyond
memorized patterns. Some studies have explored machine-learning-driven
theorem discovery \[[300](#bib.bib300)\], and FunSearch
\[[301](#bib.bib301)\] demonstrated the potential for LLMs to contribute
to open problems. However, bridging the gap between automated reasoning
and genuine mathematical discovery remains a major research frontier.

##### Application in Education

Mathematical LLMs have significant potential as tutors and educational
tools. However, effective deployment in classrooms requires addressing
pedagogical challenges such as adaptive explanations and interactive
engagement. Socratic questioning methods \[[124](#bib.bib124),
[302](#bib.bib302)\] have been proposed to improve student
understanding. Future research should focus on customizing LLMs to
different learning styles and integrating them into structured
educational frameworks.

##### Data Scarcity

Training data plays a crucial role in the performance of mathematical
LLMs \[[303](#bib.bib303)\]. While instruction-building methods have
explored dataset construction using LLMs, current efforts rely heavily
on limited datasets such as GSM8K \[[142](#bib.bib142)\] and MATH
\[[94](#bib.bib94)\]. Expanding high-quality datasets and constructing
multi-modal mathematical training data will be key to further
improvements.

## XII Conclusion

This survey has explored the landscape of mathematical reasoning and
optimization using Large Language Models (LLMs), highlighting their
rapid advancements, core methodologies, and diverse applications. LLMs
have demonstrated significant progress in solving mathematical word
problems, theorem proving, and symbolic computation, primarily due to
techniques such as Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, instruction tuning,
reinforcement learning, and tool-augmented inference. While
autoregressive and non-autoregressive language models have improved
mathematical comprehension, the integration of structured reasoning
techniques and hybrid neural-symbolic approaches remains a crucial
research direction.

Beyond theoretical mathematical reasoning, we examined the role of LLMs
in optimization and control, including their applications in
mixed-integer programming (MIP), linear quadratic control (LQC), climate
dataset analysis, and multi-agent optimization frameworks. The case
studies on Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS) optimization and
interconnected tank control illustrate how LLMs can assist in
formulating optimization problems, generating constraints, and enhancing
decision-making through fuzzy logic and reinforcement learning.
Similarly, multi-agent systems, such as AutoGen-based LP solvers,
provide a promising avenue for intelligent and adaptive optimization
workflows.

Despite these advancements, several challenges persist, including
faithfulness in reasoning, multi-modal integration, handling
uncertainty, evaluation consistency, and fostering creative mathematical
discovery. Additionally, the gap between automated theorem proving and
genuine mathematical insight remains a major research frontier. While
LLMs have shown potential in assisting with optimization tasks, their
reliability and interpretability must be further refined to ensure
robustness in real-world applications.

Future research should focus on improving the interpretability of LLMs,
enhancing their ability to perform rigorous mathematical proofs, and
developing seamless integrations with existing solvers and symbolic
computation frameworks. Moreover, expanding high-quality datasets,
refining training paradigms, and adopting hybrid neuro-symbolic
architectures will be essential in pushing the boundaries of AI-driven
mathematical reasoning and optimization. As LLMs continue to evolve,
their synergy with formal mathematical methods, scientific computing,
and engineering applications will play a pivotal role in advancing AI’s
capabilities in structured problem-solving.

prompt = """

We want to optimize the operation of a Battery Energy Storage System
(BESS). The goal is to minimize the operational cost by determining the
charge/discharge schedule for the battery over a 10-hour period.

\### Problem Definition:

The problem involves scheduling the charging and discharging of a
battery to minimize the operational cost while adhering to constraints.

1.  \*\*Battery Parameters\*\*:

- Maximum capacity (SOC_max): 100 kWh

- Initial state of charge (SOC_0): 50 kWh

- Charging efficiency ($`\eta`$): 90%

- Discharging efficiency (1/$`\eta`$): 90%

- Maximum charge/discharge power: 20 kW

2. \*\*Electricity Prices and Revenue\*\*:

- Electricity price (C_t, \$/kWh): \[20, 25, 15, 10, 30, 35, 40, 20,
  15, 10\]

- Revenue from discharging (R_t, \$/kWh): \[30, 35, 25, 20, 40, 45, 50,
  30, 25, 20\]

3. \*\*Constraints\*\*:

- Battery capacity: \\0 \\leq SOC(t) \\leq SOC\_{max}\\

- Power limits: \\0 \\leq P\_{charge}(t) \\leq P\_{charge}^{max}\\, \\0
  \\leq P\_{discharge}(t) \\leq P\_{discharge}^{max}\\

- Energy balance: \\SOC(t+1) = SOC(t) + $`\eta`$ \\cdot
  P\_{charge}(t) - \\frac{1}{$`\eta`$} \\cdot P\_{discharge}(t)\\

- No simultaneous charging/discharging: \\P\_{charge}(t) \\cdot
  P\_{discharge}(t) = 0\\

4. \*\*Objective Function\*\*:

- Minimize \\Z = \\sum\_{t=1}^{10} (C_t \\cdot P\_{charge}(t) - R_t
  \\cdot P\_{discharge}(t))\\

\### Fuzzy Logic Rules:

1. If the electricity price \\C_t\\ is high, prioritize discharging the
   battery.

2. If the battery SOC is below 30%, limit discharging and prioritize
   charging.

3. If \\C_t\\ is low, delay charging unless the SOC is critically low.

4. Avoid frequent switching between charge and discharge states.

\### Requirements:

1. Use fuzzy logic rules to guide decisions dynamically during
   optimization.

2. Reformulate constraints or priorities based on the fuzzy logic rules.

3. Solve the optimization problem and provide:

- A step-by-step explanation of how fuzzy logic influenced the
  solution.

- The final optimal charge/discharge schedule (\\P\_{charge}(t)\\,
  \\P\_{discharge}(t)\\) and state of charge (\\SOC(t)\\) for each hour.

- The total minimized operational cost.

\### Expected Output:

1. An explanation of how fuzzy logic rules were applied in each time
   step.

2. The optimal charge/discharge schedule over 10 hours.

3. The total minimized operational cost of the system.

"""

| Hour | $`C_{t}`$ | $`R_{t}`$ | Fuzzy Action     | $`P_{\text{charge}}(t)`$ | $`P_{\text{discharge}}(t)`$ | $`SOC(t)`$ | Comment                                       |
| ---- | --------- | --------- | ---------------- | ------------------------ | --------------------------- | ---------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| 1    | 20        | 30        | Slight discharge | 0                        | 5                           | 45         | Moderate net revenue.                         |
| 2    | 25        | 35        | Discharge        | 0                        | 10                          | 35         | Price is higher, good to discharge.           |
| 3    | 15        | 25        | Idle/Wait        | 0                        | 0                           | 35         | Price is moderate, fuzzy logic suggests wait. |
| 4    | 10        | 20        | Charge           | 15                       | 0                           | 48.5       | Low cost, beneficial to charge.               |
| 5    | 30        | 40        | Discharge        | 0                        | 10                          | 39.5       | Price is higher again, discharge.             |
| 6    | 35        | 45        | Discharge        | 0                        | 15                          | 26.0       | Very high price, discharge more.              |
| 7    | 40        | 50        | Discharge        | 0                        | 15                          | 12.5       | Highest price, maximize discharge.            |
| 8    | 20        | 30        | Charge           | 10                       | 0                           | 21.5       | Price moderate, partial recharge.             |
| 9    | 15        | 25        | Charge           | 15                       | 0                           | 35.0       | Low cost, continue charging.                  |
| 10   | 10        | 20        | Charge           | 10                       | 0                           | 44.0       | Low cost, end at comfortable SOC.             |

prompt = f"""

We aim to control the water levels in two interconnected tanks to
achieve desired levels while minimizing a quadratic cost function. The
system is represented as a second-order dynamical system using
state-space representation.

The current time step is {t}. The system state is:

- Tank 1 water level: x1(t) = {x\[0\]:.2f}

- Tank 2 water level: x2(t) = {x\[1\]:.2f}

The desired water levels are:

- Tank 1: x1_desired = {x_desired\[0\]:.2f}

- Tank 2: x2_desired = {x_desired\[1\]:.2f}

System parameters:

- State transition matrix A:

{A}

- Control input matrix B:

{B}

- Quadratic cost function weights:

Q1 = {Q\[0\]}, Q2 = {Q\[1\]}, R = {R}

- Maximum pump flow rate: u_max = {u_max}

Based on the improved fuzzy logic rules:

1. If x1 \> x1_desired, decrease the pump flow rate u(t) proportionally
   to the error (x1 - x1_desired).

2. If x2 \< x2_desired, increase the pump flow rate u(t) proportionally
   to the error (x2_desired - x2).

3. If x1 and x2 are close to the desired levels, reduce the rate of
   change in u(t) to avoid oscillations.

4. Balance the control action between x1 and x2 deviations to prioritize
   the larger deviation.

5. Minimize the quadratic cost function:

J = Q1 \* (x1 - x1_desired)^2 + Q2 \* (x2 - x2_desired)^2 + R \* u(t)^2.

6. Ensure the control input u(t) stays within the range \[-u_max,
   u_max\].

Provide the control input u(t) for this time step. Ensure u(t) is within
\[-u_max, u_max\].

"""

| Time (s) | $`x_{1}`$ (Tank 1 Level) | $`x_{2}`$ (Tank 2 Level) | $`u`$ (Control Input) |
| -------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------ | --------------------- |
| 0.00     | 0.93                     | 0.50                     | 0.00                  |
| 1.00     | 0.86                     | 0.50                     | 0.00                  |
| 2.00     | 0.80                     | 0.49                     | 0.00                  |
| 3.00     | 0.79                     | 0.48                     | 0.50                  |
| 4.00     | 0.74                     | 0.47                     | 0.00                  |
| 5.00     | 0.69                     | 0.46                     | 0.00                  |
| 6.00     | 0.64                     | 0.45                     | 0.00                  |
| 7.00     | 0.60                     | 0.44                     | 0.00                  |
| 8.00     | 0.56                     | 0.42                     | 0.00                  |
| 9.00     | 0.53                     | 0.41                     | 0.00                  |

Below are location-wise \*\*extended\*\* statistical features for wind
speeds and pressure data

collected over a 1-year period (2928 time steps at 3-hour intervals,
across 232 locations).

The following metrics are provided for each location:

• Mean • Median • Standard Deviation

• Variance • Min, Max • Range

• 25th Percentile • 75th Percentile • Interquartile Range (IQR)

• Skewness • Kurtosis • Coefficient of Variation

Overall (all locations, all time steps) aggregates include:

• Overall Mean Wind Speed: {overall_mean_ws:.3f}

• Overall Mean Pressure: {overall_mean_p:.3f}

Sample of per-location extended stats (first {num_locations_to_show}
locations):

{stats_string}

Using these extended statistics, please address the following:

1. \*\*Distribution & Patterns\*\*

- Comment on any notable patterns in mean, standard deviation, range,
  or skewness for wind speed and pressure.

- Identify whether certain locations exhibit heavy tails or extreme
  values (based on kurtosis) and any implications for site selection or
  forecasting.

2. \*\*Optimal Placement & Time Periods\*\*

- Explain how the observed patterns could inform optimal turbine
  placement.

- Highlight which time periods or seasons might yield the highest
  energy output.

3. \*\*Data Quality & Transformations\*\*

- Suggest if any data transformations (e.g., log-transform, outlier
  removal, normalization) might be warranted based on skewness/kurtosis.

- Propose additional data (e.g., temperature, humidity, topographic)
  that could refine site-selection or energy forecasting models.

4. \*\*Further Analysis\*\*

- Discuss whether metrics like coefficient of variation or range reveal
  anomalies/outliers needing investigation.

- If relevant, suggest how these statistics could guide more advanced
  modeling approaches.

1. \*\*Distribution & Patterns\*\*

\* The mean wind speed of 3.526 m/s is relatively low, which indicates
that the wind resource at these locations is not very strong.

\* The standard deviation values range from 1.412 to 2.059 m/s, with an
overall standard deviation of 1.690 m/s. This indicates that the wind
speeds vary significantly across different locations and time periods.

\* The range of wind speeds varies from 2.507 m/s to 10.657 m/s, with an
overall range of 8.162 m/s. This suggests that some locations experience
stronger winds than others.

\* The skewness values range from -0.219 to 0.938, with an overall
skewness of 0.334. This indicates that the wind speed data is slightly
skewed towards positive values.

\* The kurtosis values range from -0.110 to 0.837, with an overall
kurtosis of 0.274. This suggests that the wind speed data is slightly
platykurtic, meaning that the distribution is relatively flat and has a
broader tail than a normal distribution.

\* The mean pressure values range from 98838.522 hPa to 100667.027 hPa,
with an overall mean of 99531.548 hPa. This indicates that the pressure
values are relatively stable across different locations and time
periods.

\* The standard deviation values range from 723.103 hPa to 1079.370 hPa,
with an overall standard deviation of 886.921 hPa. This suggests that
the pressure values vary significantly across different locations and
time periods.

\* The range of pressure values varies from 5102.595 hPa to 6082.317
hPa, with an overall range of 5299.912 hPa. This indicates that some
locations experience larger pressure fluctuations than others.

\* The skewness values range from -0.118 to 0.207, with an overall
skewness of 0.009. This suggests that the pressure data is nearly
symmetric.

\* The kurtosis values range from -0.118 to 0.443, with an overall
kurtosis of 0.108. This indicates that the pressure data is slightly
platykurtic, meaning that the distribution is relatively flat and has a
broader tail than a normal distribution.

Based on these observations, it appears that the wind speed data
exhibits significant variability across different locations and time
periods, with some locations experiencing stronger winds than others.
The pressure data is relatively stable, but some locations experience
larger pressure fluctuations than others. The kurtosis values suggest
that the wind speed and pressure data are slightly platykurtic, which
may indicate that the data is influenced by extreme values or outliers.

2. \*\*Optimal Placement & Time Periods\*\*

\* The observed patterns in wind speed and pressure can inform optimal
turbine placement by identifying locations with stronger wind speeds and
more stable pressure conditions.

\* The data suggests that certain seasons or time periods might yield
higher energy output, as wind speed and pressure conditions vary
throughout the year. For example, locations with stronger winds during
the winter months might be more suitable for wind turbine installation.

3. \*\*Data Quality & Transformations\*\*

\* Based on the skewness and kurtosis values, it appears that no data
transformations are necessary for wind speed data. However, for pressure
data, a log-transform or normalization might be useful to reduce the
impact of extreme values and improve the distribution of the data.

\* Additional data such as temperature, humidity, and topographic
information could refine site-selection or energy forecasting models by
providing more context about the local environment and its impact on
wind and pressure conditions.

4. \*\*Further Analysis\*\*

\* The coefficient of variation and range metrics can reveal anomalies
or outliers in the data that necessitate further investigation. For
example, locations with high coefficient of variation values may
indicate unstable wind conditions or outliers in the data.

\* These statistics could guide more advanced modeling approaches, such
as using machine learning algorithms to identify patterns and
relationships in the data that are not apparent through simple
descriptive statistics.
